\int_{B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})}|\operatorname{H}[\widetilde{g}_{i}]|\leq C{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\theta_{j_{2}}^{-\beta}+C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\frac{1}{5}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{2}\leq C({\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2}){\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\beta(2-\gamma)}+C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\frac{1}{5}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{2}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2-2\beta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{2}\,,

(108)

as long as
k
is large enough.

We notice that at scale
{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
the estimate (
108
) is sharp (as, even in the absence of bad cubes, the Wang-Wei estimates do not allow to improve this).
One can see (similarly as above) that this estimate can imply (
103
) at most for
\rho\gg{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
. To go below this “critical threshold”—as required later in our proofs—we need to run a dichotomy argument.

Step 3.
Proof of (
105
).

Case 1.
Assume that
|B_{1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap B_{3{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/4}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})|\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\frac{1}{4}}
; we claim that
N_{\theta}\leq\theta^{-1}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/4}
for
\theta\in(\frac{1}{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}},{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}]
.

Indeed, by assumption
N_{\frac{1}{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\frac{1}{4}}
; since the property
N_{\theta_{2}}\leq CN_{\theta_{1}}
for
0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\leq 1
is always true
26
26

26

This can be seen using first
Lemma

5.4
, which gives
\{B_{5\theta_{1}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{Q_{1}}
with
Q_{1}\leq CN_{\theta_{1}}
such that
B_{\theta_{1}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\mathcal{Z}\cap B_{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}}))\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{Q_{1}}B_{5\theta_{1}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i})
, and then enlarging these balls to cover
B_{\theta_{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\mathcal{Z}\cap B_{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}}))
.
, we find that
N_{\theta}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\frac{1}{4}}
for
\theta\in[\frac{1}{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}},{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}]
as well, which using
\theta\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}
gives
N_{\theta}\leq\theta^{-1}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/4}
.

Then, choosing
j_{1}
with
\theta_{j_{1}}\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}\leq\theta_{j_{1}-1}
in (
107
), using
N_{\theta}\leq\theta^{-1}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/4}
instead of
N_{\theta}\leq\theta^{-1-\beta}
we find

\int_{\{d\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\}\cap B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}}^{\prime})}|\operatorname{H}[\widetilde{g}_{i}]|\leq C{\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/4}\log{\theta_{j_{2}}}+C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\frac{1}{5}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{2}\leq C{\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\frac{1}{5}}\,.

Since obviously
B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\subset\{d\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\}\cap B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}}^{\prime})
, we conclude (
105
) in this case.

Case 2.
Assume we are not in Case 1, so that
|B_{1}(\mathcal{Z})\cap B_{3{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/4}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})|>{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\frac{1}{4}}
instead; we claim that
c\leq{\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\frac{1}{4}}
.

Indeed, by (
59
) with
R={\widetilde{R}_{k}}
and
n=4
, we deduce that
\frac{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-1/4}}{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}\leq C{\bf H}_{4{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{2}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})
, which combined with
{\bf H}_{4{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{2}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})\leq 2{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2}
from
Lemma

3.18
shows
c\leq{\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\frac{1}{4}}
.

Then, computing as in (
108
) we directly obtain an extra factor
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/4}
in the second inequality, so that in particular

\int_{\{d\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1/2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\}\cap B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}}^{\prime})}|\operatorname{H}[\widetilde{g}_{i}]|\leq C({\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+1/4}){\widetilde{R}_{k}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\beta(2-\gamma)}+C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\frac{1}{5}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{2}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+1/4-2\beta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{2}\,.

This gives (
105
) as before as long as
1/4-2\beta\geq 1/5
, which is precisely the condition
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{40}]
.
∎

7.3
Improvement for each layer

Proposition 7.8
.

Fix
\chi\in(0,\frac{1}{20}]
,
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{40}]
and
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{40}]
. Given any
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in B_{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/8}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})\cap\mathcal{Z}
, there are coefficients
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for every
i\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}
such that, setting
\ell_{i}:=a_{i}\cdot(x^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})+b_{i}
, we have that

\frac{1}{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}\fint_{B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}\big|\widetilde{g}_{i}(x^{\prime})-\ell_{i}\big|\,dx\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2},\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{for every}\hskip 10.00002pti\in\{1,...,K_{*}\},

(109)

provided
k
is large enough.

We first need a standard lemma, which we prove in
Appendix

C
.

Lemma 7.9

(
Harmonic approximation).

Let
p>1
and
d\geq 0
. Given
\lambda>0
, there exists
\delta=\delta(\lambda,n,p,d)>0
such that the following holds.

Let
v\in C^{2}(B_{1/\delta}^{\prime})
,
B_{1/\delta}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, be such that

\begin{split}\rho^{2}\fint_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}}|{\rm div}(A\nabla v)|<\delta\quad\text{and}\quad\fint_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}}|v|&\leq\rho^{d+1/2}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq\rho\leq\tfrac{1}{\delta}\,,\end{split}

where
A\in C^{1}(B_{1/\delta}^{\prime})
and
|A(x^{\prime})-1|\leq\delta
.

Then,

\int_{B_{1}^{\prime}}|v-p_{d}|\,dx\leq\lambda,

where
p_{d}
is a harmonic polynomial of degree
\leq d
and such that
\|p_{d}\|_{L^{1}(B_{1})}\leq|B_{1}^{\prime}|
.

Proof of
Proposition

7.8
.

We will show the existence of some
\bar{C}\geq 1
such that the following holds:
Let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in B_{\frac{1}{4}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})
. Fix
i\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}
. Then, we have the algebraic decay

\min_{a_{i}\in B_{1}^{\prime},\,b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{R}\fint_{B_{R}^{\prime}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}\big|\widetilde{g}_{i}(x^{\prime})-a_{i}\cdot(x^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})-b_{i}\big|\,dx^{\prime}\leq\bar{C}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}\left(\frac{R}{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}\right)^{1/2}\,,

(110)

for all
R\in\left[{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}},\frac{1}{8}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\right]
.

The desired statement immediately follows.

Let us denote
R_{l}:=2^{-l}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
. We assume that (
110
) holds for
R=R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},\dots,R_{l}
and we will show it holds for
R\in[R_{l+1},R_{l}]
as well, as long as
l\geq l_{0}
for some
l_{0}
universal to be chosen, and
2^{-l}\geq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}
. Notice that, up to making
\bar{C}
larger depending only on
l_{0}
, we can always assume the statement holds up to
R_{l_{0}}
indeed, since (ii) in
Proposition

7.4
shows that

\fint_{B_{\frac{1}{4}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}}^{\prime})}\big|\widetilde{g}_{i}(x^{\prime})-({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})_{n}\big|\,dx\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\,.

(111)

Denote
h:=\widetilde{g}_{i}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}+x^{\prime})
in what follows. By assumption, for every
1\leq m\leq l
there are
a_{m},b_{m}
such that

\frac{1}{R_{m}}\fint_{B_{R_{m}}^{\prime}}|h-a_{m}\cdot x^{\prime}-b_{m}|\leq\bar{C}2^{-m/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}\,.

(112)

By the triangle inequality,

|a_{m}-a_{m+1}|+\frac{1}{R_{m}}|b_{m}-b_{m+1}|\leq C_{1}\bar{C}2^{-m/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{therefore}\hskip 10.00002pt|a_{m}-a_{l}|+\frac{1}{R_{m}}|b_{m}-b_{l}|\leq C_{2}\bar{C}2^{-m/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}.

Then, for
l-l_{0}\leq m\leq l
, (
112
) transforms into

\frac{1}{R_{m}}\fint_{B_{R_{m}}^{\prime}}|h-a_{l}\cdot x^{\prime}-b_{l}|\leq C_{3}\bar{C}2^{-m/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}},

for some universal
C_{3}
. If we define

\tilde{h}_{l}(x^{\prime}):=\frac{h(R_{l}x^{\prime})-R_{l}\,a_{l}\cdot x^{\prime}-b_{l}}{C_{3}\bar{C}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}2^{-l/2}R_{l}},

we get

\fint_{B_{2^{l-m}}^{\prime}}|\tilde{h}_{l}|\leq\frac{2^{-m/2}R_{m}}{2^{-l/2}R_{l}}=2^{3/2(l-m)},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{or}\hskip 10.00002pt\fint_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}}|\tilde{h}_{l}|\leq C_{4}\rho^{3/2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\rho\in(1,2^{l_{0}})$.}

(113)

On the other hand, letting
A=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla h|^{2}}}
,
Proposition

7.6
and (iii) in
Proposition

7.4
give that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}R_{m}^{2}\fint_{B_{R_{m}}^{\prime}}|{\rm div}(A\nabla h)|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\frac{1}{10}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{where}\hskip 10.00002pt|A(x^{\prime})-1|\leq o_{k}(1)\,,

which letting
\widetilde{A}_{l}(x^{\prime}):=A(R_{l}x^{\prime})
and rescaling gives that

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\fint_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}}|{\rm div}(\widetilde{A}_{l}\nabla\tilde{h}_{l})|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\frac{1}{10}}2^{3l/2}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\frac{1}{40}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{where}\hskip 10.00002pt|\widetilde{A}_{l}(x^{\prime})-1|\leq o_{k}(1)\,.

We have used that
2^{3/2l}\leq({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\chi})^{3/2}\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\frac{3}{40}}
for
\chi\in(0,\frac{1}{20}]
and
k
large enough in the last inequality.

By
Lemma

7.9
with
d=1
, given
\lambda>0
, for
k
and
l_{0}
large enough we have that
\int_{B_{1}^{\prime}}|\tilde{h}_{l}-a\cdot x^{\prime}-b|\leq\lambda
, for some
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
. Scaling back, we have

\frac{1}{R_{l}}\fint_{B_{R_{l}}^{\prime}}|h(x)-a_{l}\cdot x^{\prime}-b_{l}-C_{3}\bar{C}2^{-l/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}a\cdot x^{\prime}-C_{3}\bar{C}R_{l}2^{-l/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}b|\,dx^{\prime}\leq C_{4}\bar{C}2^{-l/2}\lambda{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}\,.

Denoting
b_{l+1}:=b_{l}+C_{3}\bar{C}R_{l}2^{-l/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}\,b
and
a_{l+1}=a_{l}+C_{3}\bar{C}2^{-l/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}\,a
, we obtain

\frac{1}{R_{l}}\fint_{B_{R_{l}}^{\prime}}|h(x)-a_{l+1}\cdot x^{\prime}-b_{l+1}|\,dx^{\prime}\leq C_{4}\bar{C}2^{-l/2}\lambda{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}.

We now choose
\lambda
small so that
C_{4}\lambda\leq\frac{1}{32}
, which in turn fixes
l_{0}
,
\bar{C}
, and a lower bound for
k
. We then get (
110
) for
R\in[R_{l+1},R_{l}]
as desired.
∎

7.4
Preservation of mean oscillation

We can interpret
Proposition

7.8
as saying that
\widetilde{g}_{i}-l_{i}
has average size
O({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+3\chi/2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})
in
B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
. We now show that
\widetilde{g}_{i}-l_{i}
still has average size
O({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+3\chi/2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})
in the much smaller ball
B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+2\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})
.

Proposition 7.10

(
Preservation of layer mean oscillation up to
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+2\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
).

Fix
\chi\in(0,\frac{1}{20}]
,
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{40}]
and
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{40}]
.

Then, we have

\fint_{B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+2\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}|\widetilde{g}_{i}-\ell_{i}|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 10.00002pti\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}\,,

(114)

provided
k
is large enough.

Proof.

We argue similarly to the proof of
Proposition

7.8
. We fix
i\in\{1,..,K_{*}\}
in all of the proof. Let
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
be given by
Proposition

7.8
, so that setting
\ell_{i}:=a_{i}\cdot(x^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})+b_{i}
we have that

\fint_{B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime})}\big|\widetilde{g}_{i}-\ell_{i}\big|\,dx\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}).

(115)

Set
w:=(\widetilde{g}_{i}-\ell_{i})(\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}+\,\cdot\,)
. To obtain (
114
), we will actually show that for some (tiny)
{\bar{\beta}}>0
and some
\bar{C}
, we have

\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\fint_{B_{r}^{\prime}}|w-c|\leq\bar{C}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\left(r/({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\right)^{\bar{\beta}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptr\in[{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+2\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}},{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}]\,.

(116)

Then, a direct application of the triangle inequality and the geometric decay in (
116
) give that

\fint_{B_{r}^{\prime}}|w|\leq C\bar{C}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptr\in[{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+2\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}},{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}]

(117)

as well, as desired.

Set
r_{l}:=2^{-l}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})
, and assume that (
116
) holds for
r=r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},\dots,r_{l}
. We will show it holds for
r\in[r_{l+1},r_{l}]
as well as long as
l\geq l_{0}
and
2^{-l}\geq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi}
.

Now, under these assumptions
27
27

27

Up to making
\bar{C}
larger depending on
l_{0}
, by (
115
) this will be the case.
, we have
c_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for any
1\leq m\leq l
,

w_{m}(x):=w(x)-c_{m}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{satisfies}\hskip 10.00002pt\fint_{B_{r_{m}}^{\prime}}|w_{m}|\leq\bar{C}2^{-{\bar{\beta}}m}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}).

(118)

The triangle inequality shows then that
|c_{l}-c_{m}|\leq C_{1}\bar{C}2^{-{\bar{\beta}}m}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})
as well, where
C_{1}
depends on
\bar{\beta}
. This gives

\fint_{B_{r_{m}}^{\prime}}|w_{l}|\leq C_{2}\bar{C}2^{-{\bar{\beta}}m}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\,,

(119)

which defining
\tilde{w}_{l}(x):=\frac{w_{l}(r_{l}x)}{C_{2}\widetilde{C}_{0}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})2^{-{\bar{\beta}}l}}
transforms (taking
\bar{\beta}<\frac{1}{2}
) into

\fint_{B_{2^{l-m}}^{\prime}}|\tilde{w}_{l}|\leq 2^{\bar{\beta}(l-m)}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{or}\hskip 10.00002pt\fint_{B_{\rho/2}^{\prime}}|\tilde{w}_{l}|\leq\rho^{1/2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for}\hskip 10.00002pt\rho\in(1,2^{l_{0}}).

(120)

Likewise,
Proposition

7.6
and (iii) in
Proposition

7.4
give that

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\fint_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}}|{\rm div}(\tilde{A}_{l}\nabla\tilde{h}_{l})|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\frac{1}{10}}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-3\chi/2}2^{\bar{\beta}l}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\frac{1}{80}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{where}\hskip 10.00002pt|\widetilde{A}_{l}(x^{\prime})-1|\leq o_{k}(1)\,.

We have used that
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-3\chi/2}2^{\bar{\beta}l}\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-3/40}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-2})^{\bar{\beta}}\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\frac{7}{80}}
up to choosing
\bar{\beta}\in(0,\frac{1}{160}]
and
k
large enough in the last inequality.

We can then apply
Lemma

7.9
with
d=0
and obtain that for any
\lambda>0
, as long as
l_{0}
and
k
are large enough we have that
\int_{B_{1}^{\prime}}|\tilde{w}_{l}-c|\,dx\leq\lambda
, for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Choosing
\lambda
small enough, after rescaling we conclude that (
116
) holds for
r\in[r_{l+1},r_{l}]
as well, concluding the proof.
∎

7.5
Approximation by a single linear function – Proof of
Proposition

3.20

Combining the previous results and the proximity of our graphs at small scales (
21
), we can now give:

Proof of
Proposition

3.20
and
Proposition

7.1
.

We will show
Proposition

7.1
, which is a strictly stronger result. To simplify the notation, after a translation we can (and do) assume that
\bar{\mathbf{z}}=0
. We divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1.
Improvement of the
|b_{i}|
.

Here is where we will use (
114
). Let
\rho={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+2\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
. By (
114
) and the fact that
\widetilde{g}_{i}\equiv g_{i}
in
\Omega_{2-\gamma}
, we deduce that

\frac{1}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}\int_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}}\big|g_{i}(x^{\prime})-a_{i}\cdot x^{\prime}-b_{i}\big|\,dx^{\prime}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}),\hskip 20.00003pti\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}.

(121)

Now, by
Lemma

6.3
we can bound (for
k
large enough)

\frac{1}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}\int_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}}\big|g_{i}(x^{\prime})\big|\,dx^{\prime}\leq o_{k}(1)\rho\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for every}\hskip 20.00003pti\in\{1,...,K_{*}\};

intuitively, all of the graphs pass inside
B_{\rho}
thanks to (
21
). Together with (
121
), then

\frac{1}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}\int_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}}\big|a_{i}\cdot x^{\prime}+b_{i}\big|\,dx^{\prime}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{for every}\hskip 10.00002pti\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{as well}.

(122)

Restricting to either
\{a_{i}\cdot x^{\prime}>0\}
or
\{a_{i}\cdot x^{\prime}<0\}
in the integral immediately gives that

\big|b_{i}\big|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\,,

(123)

as long as
|B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}|\geq 0.9|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|
. But this is indeed true: Using (
80
) (and the notation there), by (
31
) we can bound

|\{d\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2-\gamma}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\}\cap B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})|\leq({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2-\gamma}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})^{3}(N_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2-\gamma}})^{-1-\beta}\leq C({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2-\gamma}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})^{3}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2-\gamma})^{-1-\beta}=C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{(2-\gamma)(2-\beta)}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{3}\,,

(124)

which for
k
large enough and
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{4}]
,
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{16}]
,
\chi\in(0,\frac{1}{20}]
is strictly smaller than
|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|=c\rho^{3}=c{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{3(1+2\chi)}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{3}
.

Step 2.
Improvement of the
|a_{j}-a_{i}|
.

Let
\rho={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
now; all of our next arguments will be at scale
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
. By (
39
),

\frac{1}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}\int_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}}\big|g_{i}(x^{\prime})-a_{i}\cdot x^{\prime}-b_{i}\big|\,dx\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}\rho,\hskip 20.00003pti\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}.

(125)

Given
i<j
we know that
g_{j}-g_{i}>0
. Applying the triangle inequality and (
125
), and then Step 1,

\frac{1}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}\int_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\cdot x^{\prime}+(b_{i}-b_{j})\,dx^{\prime}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}\rho\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{thus}\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{1}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}\int_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\cdot x^{\prime}\,dx^{\prime}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}\rho\,.

Assuming that
a_{i}\neq a_{j}
, setting
A:=B_{\rho}^{\prime}\cap\{\frac{a_{i}-a_{j}}{|a_{i}-a_{j}|}\cdot x^{\prime}\geq\frac{\rho}{10}\}
we find that

\frac{|A\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}|}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}|a_{i}-a_{j}|\frac{\rho}{10}\leq\frac{1}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}\int_{A\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\cdot x^{\prime}\,dx\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}\rho\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{or}\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{|A\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}|}{|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|}|a_{i}-a_{j}|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}\,.

Arguing exactly as in the end of Step 1, we see that
|A\cap\Omega_{2-\gamma}|\geq 0.9|A|=c|B_{\rho}^{\prime}|
for
k
large enough, thus we conclude that

|a_{i}-a_{j}|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}\,.

(126)

Step 3.
Conclusion.

Steps 1 and 2 show in particular that
\frac{1}{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}\fint_{B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}(\bar{y}^{\prime})}|\ell_{i}-\ell_{1}|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}
for every
i\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}
, so that simply setting
\ell:=\ell_{1}
we see that (
109
) transforms into (
79
), showing
Proposition

7.1
. Since (by
Proposition

7.4
) we have that
g_{i}\equiv\widetilde{g}_{i}
in
\Omega_{2-\gamma}
, then (
39
) immediately follows, proving
Proposition

3.20
as desired.
∎

8
Conclusion of argument

For simplicity, let us fix
\chi=\frac{1}{20}
,
\beta=\frac{1}{40}
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{40}
in what follows.

8.1
Improvement of Allen–Cahn excess and density deficit

We want to transform the
L^{1}
bounds from
Proposition

3.20
into
L^{\infty}
ones, for which we first need:

Lemma 8.1

(
Clean cylinder).

There exists
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in B_{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/8}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})\cap\mathcal{Z}
with the following property: There is some
{\bf\bar{y}}^{\prime}
such that

{\rm dist}({\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}},[\mathcal{Z}\cap B_{{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})]^{\prime})=|{\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}|=\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\,.

Proof.

Using (
80
) and (
31
), we can bound

|\{d\leq\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\}\cap B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})|\leq(\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})^{3}(N_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}})^{-1-\beta}\leq C(\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})^{3}(\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi})^{-1-\beta}=C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{(2-\beta)\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{3}\,.

For
k
large enough this is strictly smaller than
|B_{\frac{1}{16}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})|=c{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{3}
, thus
\{d>\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\}\cap B_{\frac{1}{16}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})\neq\emptyset
. This clearly means that there is
{\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}}\in\{d=\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\}\cap B_{\frac{1}{16}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})
as well, and then we can choose some
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
such that
|{\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}|=d({\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}})=\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
.
∎

Our main result here is:

Proposition 8.2

(
Improvement of total excess and density deficit).

There exist
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
and
{\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}}
(given by
Lemma

8.1
) such that the following holds. Let
\ell
be given by
Proposition

3.20
(applied with this
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
). Setting
{\bf\bar{y}}:=({\bf\bar{y}}^{\prime},\ell({\bf\bar{y}}^{\prime}))
, then—for
k
large enough—
{\bf\bar{y}}\in B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})
. Moreover, we have:

•

Height excess bound:
{\bf H}_{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})/64}^{2}({\bf\bar{y}})\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\chi}
.

•

Density deficit bound:
K_{*}-C\left[\frac{\varepsilon_{k}^{1+4\chi/3}}{\theta}\right]^{2}\leq{\bf M}_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\bf\bar{y}})\leq K_{*}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for every}\hskip 10.00002pt\theta\in[{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}},{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}/8]
.

Proof.

The fact that
{\bf\bar{y}}\in B_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})
for
k
large enough follows easily from
Proposition

3.20
and
Lemma

6.3
.

Step 1.
Improvement of
{\bf H}
.

From
Proposition

3.20
and
|{\bf\bar{y}}^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\prime}|=\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
, for each
i\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}
we have
\frac{1}{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}\fint_{B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}})}|g_{i}-\ell|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}
.

Since
B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}})
is a (projected) good ball, by elliptic estimates (using (
16
) with
R=\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
) we can upgrade this into

\frac{1}{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}|g_{i}-\ell|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in}\hskip 10.00002ptB_{\frac{1}{4}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}({\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}}).

Set
{\bf\bar{y}}_{n}:=\ell({\bf\bar{y}^{\prime}})
; choosing an appropriate Euclidean frame, we can assume that
{\bf\bar{y}}=0
and
l\equiv 0
(up to restricting to a smaller cylinder). Then

\displaystyle\{u=0\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{K_{*}}{\rm graph}\,g_{i}\subset\{|x_{n}|\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{in the cylinder}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{C}_{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})/6}\,.

Using the second bullet in
Lemma

4.9
(since
c\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\ll{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/3}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})
) this can be upgraded to:

\{|u|\leq 0.9\}\subset\{|x_{n}|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{in}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{C}_{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})/8}.

We can then apply
Lemma

4.4
with
\delta={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}
and
\lambda=1/4
. Choosing
\chi>0
small enough so that—since
cR_{k}^{-1}\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2}
by (
60
)—we have
\delta^{2}\gg{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{(2-\chi)(3/2)}\geq c({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})^{-2(1-\lambda)}
, for
k
large, we find that
{\bf H}_{\frac{1}{64}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}^{2}(\bar{\bf y})\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\chi}
.

Step 2.
Largest cylinder.

Fix
\theta\in[{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}},{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}/8]
, and set
\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}:={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/3}\gg{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}
. We want to consider a cylinder in
B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}
containing as much of the area of our graphs as possible: Set
\mathcal{C}^{\theta}:=B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}\times[-\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}),\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})]
(recall ), where

a_{\theta}=\sqrt{\theta^{2}-[\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}]^{2}}=\theta\sqrt{1-[\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}}{\theta}]^{2}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{thus}\hskip 10.00002pt[a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}]^{2}+[\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})]^{2}=\theta^{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}^{2}\,.

Then, we see that

\bigcup_{i}{\rm graph}\,g_{i}|_{B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}}\subset\{|x_{n}|\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi/2}({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\}\cap\mathcal{C}^{\theta}\subset B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/8}\,.

Moreover, observe that

|B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|=|B_{\theta\sqrt{1-\Big[\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}}{\theta}\Big]^{2}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|=|B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|\left(1-\Big[\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}}{\theta}\Big]^{2}\right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\geq|B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|\left(1-C\Big[\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}}{\theta}\Big]^{2}\right)\,.

(127)

Step 3.
Slicing argument—we show that each sheet of
\{u=0\}
,
x_{n}=g_{i}(x^{\prime})
, forces a contribution of almost
\frac{|B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|}{\omega_{n-1}}
(with a quantified error) to the total A–C energy of
u
in
B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}
.

Let
\Lambda:=\frac{1+3/4}{4}\sqrt{2}\log{\frac{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}{8}}
, and define for every
i\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}
the “transition regions”

L_{i}^{-}:=\left\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):x^{\prime}\in B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}\,,\,x_{n}\in\left(g_{i}(x^{\prime})-\Lambda,g_{i}(x^{\prime})\right)\right\}

and

L_{i}^{+}:=\left\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):x^{\prime}\in B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}\,,\,x_{n}\in\left(g_{i}(x^{\prime}),g_{i}(x^{\prime})+\Lambda\right)\right\}\,.

They are all contained in
\mathcal{C}^{\theta}
. The point is that, by (
17
), we know that
g_{i+1}-g_{i}\geq\Lambda
, thus
L_{i}^{+}\cap L_{i+1}^{-}=\emptyset
for every
1\leq i<K_{*}
(eventually in
k
). This shows that the transition regions are all disjoint (for
k
large enough).

Fix
i\in\{1,...,K_{*}\}
; applying Fubini and
|\nabla u|^{2}\geq u_{x_{n}}^{2}
, we see that

\displaystyle\sigma_{n-1}\mathcal{E}(u,L_{i}^{+})=\int_{B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{g_{i}(x^{\prime})}^{g_{i}(x^{\prime})+\Lambda}dx_{n}\,\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]\geq\int_{B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{g_{i}(x^{\prime})}^{g_{i}(x^{\prime})+\Lambda}dx_{n}\,\left[\frac{u_{x_{n}}^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]\,.

Define the 1D function
f_{x^{\prime}}(t):=u\left(x^{\prime},g_{i}(x^{\prime})+t\right)
,
t\in[0,\Lambda]
. Then, letting
t:=x_{n}-g_{i}(x^{\prime})
in the integral,

\displaystyle\sigma_{n-1}\mathcal{E}(u,L_{i}^{+})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}dt\,\left[\frac{(\frac{d}{dt}f_{x^{\prime}})^{2}(t)}{2}+W(f_{x^{\prime}}(t))\right]\,.

Fixed
x^{\prime}
, the inner integral is (up to a factor
\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}
) precisely the A–C energy in
[0,\Lambda]
of the 1D function
f_{x^{\prime}}(t)
. Moreover,
f_{x^{\prime}}(0)=u(x^{\prime},g_{i}(x^{\prime}))=0
. Now,
f_{x^{\prime}}(t)
is not necessarily a solution to A–C in 1D; on the other hand, letting
\kappa=3/4
and
R=\frac{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}{8}
, its A–C energy is at least

P(\kappa,R):=\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}_{1D}\left(v,\frac{1+\kappa}{4}\sqrt{2}\log{R}\right):v\in C_{c}^{1}([0,\frac{1+\kappa}{4}\sqrt{2}\log{R}])\text{ and }v(0)=0\right\},

where we denote

\mathcal{E}_{1D}(v,r)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}\int_{0}^{r}\frac{1}{2}(v^{\prime}(t))^{2}+W(v(t))\,dt\,,

and the infimum is attained by a true 1D Allen–Cahn solution. In
Proposition

D.1
, it is shown that
P(\kappa,R)\geq\frac{1}{2}-CR^{-(1+\kappa)}
, which since
{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\geq c{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-2}
(by (
60
)) shows that
P(\kappa,R)\geq\frac{1}{2}-C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{(2-\chi)(1+\kappa)}\geq\frac{1}{2}-C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{3}
.

Combined with the above and (
127
), we find that

\displaystyle\omega_{n-1}\mathcal{E}(u,L_{i}^{+})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{x^{\prime}\in B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}}P\left(\kappa,R\right)\,dx^{\prime}\geq\left|B_{a_{\theta}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}\right|\left[\frac{1}{2}-C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{3}\right]\geq|B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|\left[\frac{1}{2}-C[\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}}{\theta}]^{2}\right]\,,

so that

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}=\frac{\omega_{n-1}}{|B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|}\mathcal{E}(u,B_{\frac{({\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}})}{8}})\geq\frac{\omega_{n-1}}{|B_{\theta{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{\prime}|}\sum_{i=1}^{K_{*}}\left[\mathcal{E}(u,L_{i}^{-})+\mathcal{E}(u,L_{i}^{+})\right]\geq K_{*}-C[\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{k}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}}{\theta}]^{2}

as desired.
∎

8.2
A monotonicity-type formula: Density deficit controls growth of excess

Definition 8.3

(
Weighted excess and density).

Let
f_{r,d}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
G_{r,d}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined by

f_{r,d}(t):=\frac{1}{c_{d}r^{d}}e^{\frac{-t^{2}}{r^{2}}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002ptG_{r,d}(x):=f_{r,d}(|x|)\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{where}\hskip 10.00002ptc_{d}=d\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{d-1}e^{-t^{2}}\,.

We set

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(x\cdot e)^{2}\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]G_{r,n+1},\hskip 10.00002pt{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}^{2}:=\frac{1}{\sigma_{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]G_{r,n-1}\,.

Moreover, we denote
{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}:=\inf_{e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e)
,
{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e,x_{0}):={\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(u(\cdot-x_{0}),e)
, and
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}^{2}(x_{0}):={\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}^{2}(u(\cdot-x_{0}))
.

The weighted versions are just as good for our purposes, up to a going to a slightly smaller scale:

Proposition 8.4

(
Improvement of weighted excess and density deficit).

There exists
{\bf\bar{y}}
(given by
Proposition

8.2
) such that the following holds. Let
\chi_{\sharp}=\frac{31}{30}\chi
. Then, we have

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{2}({\bf\bar{y}})\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\chi_{\sharp}/2}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002ptK_{*}-{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\chi_{\sharp}/2}\leq{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\bf\bar{y}})\leq{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{\infty}=K_{*}

(128)

for
k
large enough.

We will show this later. We first state the crucial monotonicity-type formula that will allow to propagate the smallness of
{\bf\widetilde{H}}
from scale
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
to the “original scale”
R_{k}
.

Theorem 8.5

(
Density deficit controls excess growth
)
.

For any
r>0
and
\lambda\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, we have
28
28

28

This result works for any A–C solution
u:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[-1,1]
, and not just for the critical solution and
n=4
.

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}\leq{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{\lambda r}+C|\log(\lambda)|^{1/2}({\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{\lambda r})^{1/2}\,.

(129)

Proof.

It suffices to show that for every
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}(e)\leq{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{\lambda r}(e)+C[1+|\log(\lambda)|^{1/2}]({\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{\lambda r})^{1/2}\,.

(130)

Up to choosing the right frame we can assume that
e=e_{n}
. Define
P:=\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]
and
Q:=\left[W(u)-\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}\right]
.

We will use the following:

•

Kernel properties:
\nabla G_{r,d}=\frac{-2}{r^{2}}G_{r,d}x\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002ptr\frac{d}{dr}G_{r,d}=-x\cdot\nabla G_{r,d}-dG_{r,d}\,.

•

Pohozaev identity
29
29

29

Proved via a standard computation using
\Delta u=W^{\prime}(u)
.
:
{\rm div}(Px)={\rm div}((x\cdot\nabla u)\nabla u)+nP-|\nabla u|^{2}
.

•

\nabla x_{n}^{2}=2x_{n}e_{n}
.

Step 1.
Pohozaev–type computations.

Observe that

\displaystyle r\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})=r\frac{d}{dr}\int x_{n}^{2}PG_{r,n+1}

\displaystyle=\int x_{n}^{2}P\left(-x\cdot\nabla G_{r}-(n+1)G_{r}\right)\,.

Integrating by parts,

\displaystyle r\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})

\displaystyle=\int\nabla x_{n}^{2}\cdot xPG_{r,n+1}+\int x_{n}^{2}{\rm div}(Px)G_{r,n+1}-(n+1)\int x_{n}^{2}PG_{r}

\displaystyle=2\int x_{n}^{2}PG_{r,n+1}+\int x_{n}^{2}\left[{\rm div}((x\cdot\nabla u)\nabla u)-P-|\nabla u|^{2}\right]G_{r,n+1}

\displaystyle=\int x_{n}^{2}QG_{r,n+1}+\int x_{n}^{2}{\rm div}((x\cdot\nabla u)\nabla u)G_{r,n+1}\,.

Integrating the new divergence term by parts, relating
G_{r,n+1}
and
G_{r,n-1}
, and using Cauchy–Schwarz we arrive at

\displaystyle r\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})

\displaystyle=\int x_{n}^{2}[Q+\frac{2}{r^{2}}(x\cdot\nabla u)^{2}]G_{r,n+1}-2\int x_{n}G_{r,n+1}(x\cdot\nabla u)u_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}}{r^{2}}\int x_{n}^{2}[Q+\frac{2}{r^{2}}(x\cdot\nabla u)^{2}]G_{r,n-1}-2c_{2}\int(x_{n}u_{n}G_{r,n+1}^{1/2})(\frac{x\cdot\nabla u}{r}G_{r,n-1}^{1/2})

\displaystyle\leq C\int[Q+\frac{2}{r^{2}}(x\cdot\nabla u)^{2}]G_{r,n-1}+C[\int x_{n}^{2}PG_{r,n+1}]^{1/2}[\int\frac{1}{r^{2}}(x\cdot\nabla u)^{2}G_{r,n-1}]^{1/2}\,.

Now, analogous computations show that

r\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{n-1}}\int[Q+\frac{2}{r^{2}}(x\cdot\nabla u)^{2}]G_{r,n-1}\,.

(131)

In particular,
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}
is monotone nondecreasing by
Lemma

2.1
. Moreover, we see that

\displaystyle r\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})\leq Cr\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}+C[{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})]^{1/2}[r\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}]^{1/2}\,.

Step 2.
Grönwall-type argument.

Given
0<a<r
, integrating we find that

\displaystyle{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})\leq{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{a}^{2}(e_{n})+C[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]+C\int_{a}^{r}[{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})]^{1/2}[\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}]^{1/2}=:{\bf A}_{r}\,.

We then see that

\frac{d}{dr}{\bf A}_{r}=C\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}+C[{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}(e_{n})]^{1/2}[r\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}]^{1/2}\leq C\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}+C[{\bf\widetilde{A}}_{r}]^{1/2}[\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}]^{1/2}\,,

thus dividing by
{\bf A}_{r}^{1/2}
and using
{\bf A}_{r}\geq C[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]
we reach
30
30

30

We can assume that
[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]>0
, as otherwise (
131
) forces
u
to be constant along rays, thus
u\equiv u(0)
.

\displaystyle 2\frac{d}{dr}{\bf A}_{r}^{1/2}\leq C\frac{\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}}{{\bf A}_{r}^{1/2}}+C[\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\frac{d}{dr}[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]}{[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]^{1/2}}+C[\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}]^{1/2}=2C\frac{d}{dr}[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]^{1/2}+C[\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dr}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}]^{1/2}\,.

Integrating and using Cauchy–Schwarz, we find that

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{r}^{1/2}-{\bf A}_{a}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]^{1/2}+C[\int_{a}^{r}\frac{1}{s}ds]^{1/2}[\int_{a}^{r}\frac{d}{ds}{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{s}ds]^{1/2}=C[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]^{1/2}+C\log^{1/2}{(r/a)}[{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a}]^{1/2}\,.

Using that
{\bf H}_{r}(e_{n})\leq{\bf A}_{r}^{1/2}
and
{\bf H}_{a}(e_{n})={\bf A}_{a}^{1/2}
, we conclude that

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}(e_{n})\leq{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{a}(e_{n})+C[1+\log^{1/2}{(r/a)}]({\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{a})^{1/2}\,,

which putting
a=\lambda r
gives (
130
).
∎

We will now show
Proposition

8.4
. We first need a couple of lemmas.

Lemma 8.6

(
Density properties).

{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}
is monotone nondecreasing. Moreover, we have the layer cake-type formula

{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t){\bf M}_{t}\,,

(132)

where
-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)=1
.

Proof.

We already discussed the monotonicity of
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}
(recall (
131
)). Moreover, we can compute

-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)=(n-1)\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-2}f_{r,n-1}(t)=\frac{n-1}{c_{n}r^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-2}e^{\frac{-t^{2}}{r^{2}}}=\frac{n-1}{c_{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-2}e^{-t^{2}}=1\,.

Finally, letting
P:=\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]
, using polar coordinates and
G_{r,d}(x)=f_{r,d}(|x|)
we can compute

\displaystyle\int PG_{r,n-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,f_{r,n-1}(t)\int_{\partial B_{t}}P=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,f_{r,n-1}(t)\frac{d}{dt}\int_{B_{t}}P=-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)\frac{1}{t^{n-1}}\int_{B_{t}}P\,,

which gives precisely (
132
).
∎

Lemma 8.7

(
Height excess properties).

We have

{\bf H}_{r}^{2}\leq C{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{r}^{2}\,.

(133)

Moreover, given
\lambda\in(0,1)
we have

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{\lambda r}^{2}\leq\lambda^{-(n+1)}C{\bf H}_{r}^{2}+Ce^{-\frac{1}{2\lambda}}{\bf M}_{\infty}\,.

(134)

Proof.

We clearly have the pointwise inequalities

\frac{1}{r^{n+1}}\chi_{B_{r}}\leq CG_{r,n+1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002ptG_{\lambda r}\leq C\frac{1}{(\lambda r)^{n+1}}\chi_{B_{r}}+C\frac{1}{(\lambda r)^{n+1}}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}r^{2}}}\chi_{B_{r}^{c}}\,.

Integrating the first one we get (
133
). Integrating the second one we get

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{\lambda r}^{2}\leq\lambda^{-(n+1)}C{\bf H}_{r}^{2}+C\lambda^{-(n+1)}\frac{1}{r^{n+1}}\int_{B_{r}^{c}}|x|^{2}\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}r^{2}}}\,,

which splitting the integral into dyadic scales and using that
{\bf M}_{R}\leq{\bf M}_{\infty}
easily gives (
134
).
∎

Proof of
Proposition

8.4
.

Up to a translation we can assume that
{\bf\bar{y}}=0
. Taking
\lambda={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\frac{1}{30}\chi}
in (
134
), by
Proposition

8.2
we find that

{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{2}\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{-\chi/6}C{\bf H}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{2}+Ce^{-{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi/60}}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\chi_{\sharp}/2}\,.

(135)

It remains to estimate
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}
.
Since
-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)=1
and
{\bf M}_{t}\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}{\bf M}_{\infty}
, it is clear that

{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{\infty}=-\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t){\bf M}_{t}={\bf M}_{\infty}=K_{*}\,.

For the lower bound, using
-\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)=1
again and the monotonicity of
{\bf M}_{t}
, given
0<a<r
we can estimate

\displaystyle K_{*}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)[K_{*}-{\bf M}_{t}]dt

\displaystyle\leq-\int_{r}^{\infty}t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)[K_{*}-{\bf M}_{r}]dt-\int_{0}^{r}t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)[K_{*}-{\bf M}_{t}]dt

\displaystyle\leq[K_{*}-{\bf M}_{r}]-\left[\int_{0}^{a}+\int_{a}^{r}\right]t^{n-1}f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)[K_{*}-{\bf M}_{t}]dt\,.

Bounding
f_{r,n-1}^{\prime}(t)\leq C\frac{t}{r^{n+1}}
in the integrals, taking
r={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
and
a={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
, and using the density lower bound from
Proposition

8.2
, we reach

\displaystyle K_{*}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}

\displaystyle\leq[K_{*}-{\bf M}_{r}]+C(a/r)^{n+1}+\frac{C}{r^{n+1}}\int_{a}^{r}t^{n}[K_{*}-{\bf M}_{t}]dt

\displaystyle\leq(\varepsilon_{k}^{1+4\chi/3}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/r)^{2}+C(a/r)^{n+1}+\frac{C}{r^{n+1}}\int_{a}^{r}t^{n}(\varepsilon_{k}^{1+4\chi/3}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/t)^{2}dt\leq(\varepsilon_{k}^{1+4\chi/3}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}/r)^{2}+C(a/r)^{n+1}\,,

thus
K_{*}-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}\leq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\chi_{\sharp}/2}
.
∎

8.3
Back to the original scale – Proof of
Theorem

1.3

Proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

Proving
Theorem

1.3
is equivalent to showing that every
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a subcritical density in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
(recall
Definition

3.1
). We assume then for contradiction that

K_{*}=\sup\{K>0\mbox{ subcritical density in }\mathbb{R}^{4}\}<\infty
.

By
Proposition

3.6
, there is then a stable solution
u:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to(-1,1)
with
{\bf M}_{\infty}=K_{*}
which is not 1D. Let
\mathcal{Z}(u)
be its bad set, defined in
Definition

3.7
; it is non-empty by
Lemma

3.8
.

Fix
\gamma=\frac{1}{4}
. Fix some
\chi\in(0,\frac{1}{20}]
,
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{40}]
and
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{40}]
.

Let
R_{k}\to\infty
and
\mathbf{z}_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}
be given by
Lemma

3.17
, with associated
\varepsilon_{k}^{2}={\bf H}_{4R_{k}}^{2}({\mathbf{z}_{k}})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.

Let
{\widetilde{R}_{k}}=\widetilde{\theta}_{k}R_{k}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}\cap B_{R_{k}}(\mathbf{z}_{k})
be given by
Lemma

3.18
, where
\tilde{\theta}_{k}\in(0,1]
, and let
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}=\widetilde{\theta}_{k}^{\beta_{\circ}(1+\alpha)}\varepsilon_{k}
.
Recall that:

•

we have
{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\to\infty
and
{\bf H}_{4{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}^{2}({{\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}}})\leq 2{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.

•

moreover (
30
) and (
31
) hold.

Combining
Proposition

8.4
and the monotonicity (by
Lemma

8.6
) of
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}
, there is some
{\bf\bar{y}}\in B_{\frac{1}{2}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})
such that

K_{*}-C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{2+\chi_{\sharp}/2}\leq{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}({\bf\bar{y}})\leq K_{*}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for every}\hskip 10.00002ptr\geq{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\,,

with
\chi_{\sharp}=\frac{31}{30}\chi
and for
k
large enough.
Applying
Theorem

8.5
with
r=4R_{k}
and
\lambda r={\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}
, and using
Proposition

8.4
once again,

\displaystyle{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{4R_{k}}({\bf\bar{y}})

\displaystyle\leq{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\bf\bar{y}})+C|\log(\lambda)|^{1/2}({\bf\widetilde{M}}_{4R_{k}}({\bf\bar{y}})-{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}({\bf\bar{y}}))^{1/2}\leq C{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi_{\sharp}/4}+C|\log(\lambda)|^{1/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{1+\chi_{\sharp}/4}\,.

Now, we can easily beat the log factor, by using (for the first and only time) that
\beta_{0}>0
: Since
\lambda=\frac{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}}{\widetilde{R}_{k}}}{4R_{k}}=\frac{1}{4}\widetilde{\theta}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{\chi_{\sharp}}
and
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}=\widetilde{\theta}_{k}^{\beta_{\circ}(1+\alpha)}\varepsilon_{k}
, we find that
|\log(\lambda)|^{1/2}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{k}}^{\chi_{\sharp}/8}\to 0
as
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
. In particular, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{4R_{k}}({\bf\bar{y}})

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\chi_{\sharp}/8}\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{for all }k\mbox{ large enough}.

But then, since
B_{R_{k}}(\mathbf{z}_{k})\subset B_{2R_{k}}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})\subset B_{4R_{k}}({\bf\bar{y}})
, together with (
133
) we find that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}={\bf H}_{R_{k}}(\mathbf{z}_{k})\leq C{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{R_{k}}({\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}})\leq C{\bf\widetilde{H}}_{4R_{k}}({\bf\bar{y}})

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon_{k}^{1+\chi_{\sharp}/8}\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{for all }k\mbox{ large enough}.

Recalling once again that
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, for
k
large enough we get a contradiction.
∎

8.4
Proof of
Theorem

1.4

Proof of
Theorem

1.4
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}:B_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}\to(-1,1)
and
\Lambda
be as in the statement, so that
{\bf M}_{1}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq\Lambda
. If
u_{\varepsilon}
were a solution on all of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
instead of just
B_{1}
, since we now known that every density is subcritical in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
we would easily conclude by
Theorem

3.2
. The only difference is that we cannot directly use
Lemma

2.3
; nevertheless, the local monotonicity formula with errors in
[
HT00
, Proposition 3.4]
gives universal
C_{1},C_{2}
such that

{\bf M}_{r}(u_{\varepsilon},x)\leq C_{1}\Lambda+C_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for every}\hskip 10.00002ptx\in B_{3/4}\,.

(136)

Moreover, by
Theorem

1.3
we know that
K:=C_{1}\Lambda+C_{2}
is a subcritical density. Then, up to using (
136
) in place of (
45
) and (
47
), the rest of the proof of
Theorem

3.2
follows exactly as written and gives the result.
∎

Appendix

Appendix A
The varifold theory for Allen–Cahn

A central insight of the classical theory is that stable solutions to
\varepsilon
-A–C converge, as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, to stable integral varifolds. We provide here in a restricted setting the most basic definitions and results underlying this convergence (in particular, we do not deal with general varifolds, and we state simpler versions of known results whenever they suffice).

Definition A.1

(
Integral varifold).

•

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open. An
integral
(or integer rectifiable)
varifold

V
is a pair
V=(\Sigma,\theta)
, where
\Sigma\subset\Omega
is an
(n-1)
-rectifiable set, and
\theta:\Sigma\to\mathbb{N}
is an
\mathcal{H}_{n-1}
–integrable function. We can naturally associate a
weight measure

\|V\|
to it by
\|V\|(A)=\frac{1}{\omega_{n-1}}\int_{A\cap\Sigma}\theta\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}
.

•

We define its support via
{\rm{\rm supp}\,}V:={\rm supp}\,\|V\|
. Its regular part
{\rm reg}\,V
is defined as the set of all points
x\in{\rm supp}\,V
such that
\Sigma
is actually a smooth hypersurface in some open neighbourhood around
x
. In particular,
{\rm reg}\,V
is open. We define
{\rm sing}\,V={\rm supp}\,V\setminus{\rm reg}\,V
.

•

We say that
V
is
stationary
in
\Omega
if
\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\|(\Phi_{X}^{t})_{\sharp}V\|(\Omega)=0
, where
X
is a smooth vector field with
{\rm supp}\,X\Subset\Omega
,
\Phi_{X}^{t}
is its flow at time
t
, and
(\Phi_{X}^{t})_{\sharp}V=(\Phi_{X}^{t}(\Sigma),\theta\circ\Phi_{X}^{-t})
. Moreover, it is called
stable
if its regular part is a stable smooth minimal hypersurface, meaning that
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}\|(\Phi_{X}^{t})_{\sharp}V\|(\Omega)\geq 0
for every
X
with
{\rm supp}\,X\Subset{\rm reg}\,V
.

Definition A.2

(
Allen–Cahn weight measure,
[
HT00
]
)
.

Given a solution
u_{\varepsilon}:\Omega\to[-1,1]
to
\varepsilon
-A–C, we define the Allen–Cahn weight measure
\|V_{\varepsilon}\|
on
\Omega
via, given
A\subset\Omega
,

\|V_{\varepsilon}\|(A):=\frac{1}{\sigma_{n-1}}\int_{-1}^{1}\sqrt{2W(t)}\,\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\{u=t\}\cap A)\,dt\,.

We are using that, by Sard’s theorem,
\{u=t\}
is a smooth submanifold for almost every
t
.

As a motivation for the definition above, note that by the coarea theorem we can write

\displaystyle\|V_{\varepsilon}\|(A)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{n-1}}\int_{A}\sqrt{2W(u_{\varepsilon})}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|\,dx\,.

Let
a:=\sqrt{\frac{2W(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}}
and
b:=\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|
. Using that
2ab=a^{2}+b^{2}-(b-a)^{2}\geq a^{2}+b^{2}-|b^{2}-a^{2}|
we readily deduce that

\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},A)-D(u_{\varepsilon},A)\leq\|V_{\varepsilon}\|(A)\leq\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},A)\,,

where
D(u_{\varepsilon},A):=\frac{1}{\sigma_{n-1}}\int_{A}\left|\frac{W(u_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}-\frac{\varepsilon|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{2}\right|\,dx
is a discrepancy term.

Huchinson and Tonegawa established a deep connection between stationary points of
\varepsilon
-A–C and integral stationary varifolds. The following is a partial statement of their results.

Theorem A.3

(
[
HT00
]
)
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}:\Omega\to[-1,1]
be a sequence of solutions to the
\varepsilon
-A–C equation, with
\varepsilon\to 0
, and assume that
\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon},\Omega)\leq\Lambda
. Define the A–C weight measures
\|V_{\varepsilon}\|
as in
Definition

A.2
.

Then, there is a subsequence
u_{\varepsilon_{k}}
with
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
, and a stationary integer rectifiable varifold
V
, such that the following hold:

•

\|V_{\varepsilon_{k}}\|\to\|V\|
as measures.

•

D(u_{\varepsilon_{k}},A)\to 0
for any
A\Subset\Omega
. In particular,

\|V\|(\Omega^{\prime})=\lim_{k}\|V_{\varepsilon_{k}}\|(\Omega^{\prime})=\lim_{k}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon_{k}}(u_{\varepsilon_{k}},\Omega^{\prime})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for any }\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega\mbox{ with }\|V\|(\partial\Omega^{\prime})=0.

(137)

•

As
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\{|u_{\varepsilon}|\leq 0.9\}\mbox{ converges locally in the Hausdorff distance sense to }{\rm spt}\,V.

(138)

We now focus on stable solutions.

Remark A.4
.

In the stable case, rescaling
Theorem

2.11
shows directly (and quantifies) the discrepancy decay.

Theorem A.5

(
[
Ton05
]
)
.

In the setting of
Theorem

A.3
, assume moreover that the
u_{\varepsilon}
are stable. Then,
V
is also stable.

Assume now furthermore that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, so that
{\rm supp}\,V
is a union of straight segments. Then
{\rm supp}\,V
is actually a union of non-intersecting straight lines.

The latter is a general property and holds also in higher dimensions, as shown by Tonegawa and Wickramasekera:

Theorem A.6

(
[
TW12
, Proposition 3.2]
)
.

In the setting of
Theorem

A.3
, assume moreover that the
u_{\varepsilon}
are stable. Assume that
0\in\Omega
and
{\rm spt}\|V\|=C\cap\Omega
, where
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a cone with
n-2
directions of translation invariance. Then
C
is a hyperplane.

Remark A.7
.

Cones
C
with
n-2
directions of translation invariance are, choosing an appropriate Euclidean coordinate frame, of the form
C_{0}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
, where
C_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a union of half-lines intersecting at the origin. In other words, they are unions of half-hyperplanes meeting along a common boundary.

The above are all the results we will use in the article. The following remark is in order:

Remark A.8
.

The motivation for showing
Theorem

A.6
in
[
TW12
]
is the following:
Wickramasekera developed a deep regularity theory for codimension
1
stable integral varifolds in
[
Wic14
]
, which shows that they cannot have branch points (i.e. flat singularities) as long as they do not have classical singularities
31
31

31

V
is said to have a classical singularity at
x\in{\rm sing}\,V
if there is some
r>0
such that
{\rm spt}\,V\cap B_{r}(x)
is a
C^{1,\alpha}
perturbation of a union of half-hyperplanes meeting along a common boundary.
either. In fact, such varifolds need to be then of optimal regularity (i.e. as regular as in the case of area-minimisers). This theory can then (by the property in
Theorem

A.6
) be applied in particular to limits of stable A–C solutions, which shows:

Theorem A.9

(
[
TW12
,
Wic14
]
)
.

In the setting of
Theorem

A.3
, assume moreover that the
u_{\varepsilon}
are stable. Then,
{\rm sing}\,V
is empty if
n\leq 7
, discrete if
n=8
, and of Hausdorff codimension at least
8
if
n\geq 9
.

We believe it is worth highlighting—as explained in
Section

3.1
—that our proof of
Theorem

1.3
does
not
use the powerful
Theorem

A.9
.

Appendix B
A self-contained theory for stable solutions

In our current arguments, the results from
[
HT00
,
TW12
]
are used only in two places:

The first instance is at the end of the proof of
Theorem

3.2
. There, we need to argue that a minimal hypersurface, obtained as a
C^{2,\alpha}
limit of (the level sets of) stable
\varepsilon
-A–C solutions which satisfy the sheeting assumptions in balls of radius one, is also stable. We appealed to
Theorem

A.5
for efficiency, but we could alternatively easily pass (
11
) to the limit to recover the stability inequality for minimal hypersurfaces, using the
C^{2,\alpha}
convergence and the fact that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\{u=u(x)\}}|^{2}(x)\leq\mathcal{A}^{2}(u)(x)
.

The second instance is in the proof of Propositions
3.9
and
3.10
, where we used Theorems
A.3
,
A.5
and
A.6
to provide a short and direct argument. We explain in what follows how to avoid their use completely.

The fact that
K_{*}\in\mathbb{N}
, which is obained in the proof of
Proposition

3.9
via
Theorem

A.3
, is not needed at that point. We can instead proceed up until
Section

6
without knowing that
K_{*}
is an integer, and only then—when proving
Proposition

3.19
—
deduce
the integrality of
K_{*}
in a straightforward way: By (
77
), we find a large “clean” ball with
{\bf M}_{\delta\rho}(\bar{x})=K_{*}+o_{k}(1)
. Moreover, applying
Lemma

4.9
, we find
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
{\bf M}_{\delta\rho}(\bar{x})=N+o_{k}(1)
. Combining both facts, obviously
K_{*}\in\mathbb{N}
. Such approach perfectly aligns with the “a-priori philosophy” that is present throughout the work.

The other properties in
Propositions

3.9
and
3.10
can be obtained (without appealing to
[
HT00
,
TW12
]
) as follows:

Alternative proof of
Propositions

3.9
and
3.10
(sketch).

This alternative proof gives all the conclusions of
Propositions

3.9
and
3.10
except the integrality of
K_{*}
(which as explained above can be deduced later in our argument).

We adhere to the same notation and setup as in the original proof (
Section

4.2
). Repeating the argument from Step 1 there, which does not use
[
HT00
,
TW12
]
, we obtain:

K_{*}-\omega(\frac{1}{R})\leq\widetilde{\bf M}_{R}(\mathbf{z})\leq K_{*}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{Z}\,,

(139)

where
\omega
is a dimensional modulus of continuity. Notice that
\widetilde{\bf M}_{R}
is the weighted excess from
Definition

8.3
.

We then continue the proof verbatim, arguing by contradiction exactly as in the paragraph before Step 2—recall that we put
\widetilde{u}_{i}(x):=u_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{i}+R_{i}x)
, and
\widetilde{u}_{i}
is a solution of
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i}
-A–C with
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i}=\frac{1}{R_{i}}
. Letting
\mathcal{Z}(u_{i})
denote the bad centers of
u_{i}
, set
\widetilde{Z}_{i}:=\frac{1}{R_{i}}(\mathcal{Z}(u_{i})-\mathbf{z}_{i})\cap B_{1}
, the sequence of rescaled (and translated) bad sets.

Construction of a spine.
Define the “minimal spine dimension”

d_{*}:={\rm min}\Big\{d\in[0,n]\cap\mathcal{N}\hskip 10.00002pt|\hskip 10.00002pt\liminf_{i\to\infty}\min_{L\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,n)}\sup_{y\in\widetilde{Z}_{i}}{\rm dist}(y,L)=0\Big\},

where
\mathrm{Gr}(d,n)
denotes the Grassmannian of
d
-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

By definition of
d_{*}
there exists a subsequence of
(u_{i_{l}})_{l\geq 0}
such that for all
l
we have:

•

There is
L\in\mathrm{Gr}(d_{*},n)
such that
\sup_{y\in\widetilde{Z}_{i_{l}}}{\rm dist}(y,L)\to 0
as
l\to\infty
.

•

There exist
d_{*}
points in
\widetilde{Z}_{i_{l}}
, labeled
y^{1}_{l},y^{2}_{l}\dots,y^{d_{*}}_{l}
, such that the gram determinant of the
d_{*}
vectors is bounded by below by the same positive number for all
l
.

By (
139
), combined with the monotonicity formula (see (
131
)) centered at
0
and at
y^{j}_{l}
, we obtain

\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{2}}(x\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}})^{2}=o(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{2}}((x-y^{j}_{l})\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}})^{2}=o(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{as }l\to\infty\,.

Given any
e\in L\cap B_{4}
, which we can then write as a linear combination of the
y_{l}^{j}
, we deduce that

\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{2}}(e\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}})^{2}=o(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{2}}((x-e)\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}})^{2}=o(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{as }l\to\infty.

(140)

From now on,
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}
will denote the natural rescaled version of the original density (exactly as in
Remark

2.2
). By the above, we can then deduce (for instance, arguing as in
[
Sim18
, pp. 116-118]
):

Lemma B.1
.

Fix
x
and
r>0
such that
B_{2r}(x)\subset B_{1}\setminus L
. Then, the following hold:

•

Dilation invariance:
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}(x)={\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}(x+\lambda(x-e))+o(1)
for any
e\in L\cap B_{1}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
.

•

Translation invariance:
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}(x)={\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}(x+e)+o(1)
for any
e\in L\cap B_{1}
.

•

Constancy in
L
:
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}(0)={\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}(e)+o(1)
for any
e\in L\cap B_{1}
.

Convergence to a hyperplane.
To conclude, we need to deduce that—up to a subsequence—there are hyperplanes
H_{i}
such that, given
r>0
, for
i
large enough the following hold:

\{|u|\leq 0.9\}\cap B_{1}\subset B_{r}(H_{i})\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{r}(u_{i},x)\geq K_{*}-o(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for every}\hskip 10.00002ptx\in H_{i}\cap B_{1}\,.

(141)

Notice that (
140
) implies
d_{*}<n
: if we had
d_{*}=n
, then (
140
) would imply
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{1}}|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|^{2}=o(1)
as
l\to\infty
, which readily gives a contradiction with lower density estimates.
We distinguish then two cases
d_{*}=n-1
and
d_{*}<n-1
.

Case
d_{*}=n-1
.
Let
s>0
. By the third bullet in
Lemma

B.1
and
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{s}(0)=K_{*}-o(1)
, we have
{\bf\widetilde{M}}_{s}(x)=K_{*}-o(1)

\forall x\in L\cap B_{1}
. The second part of (
141
) follows.

To see the first one, let
r>0
. It is easy to see (by a cover of
L\cap B_{1}
with disjoint balls of different radii) that
\mathcal{E}(u_{i},B_{r/2}(L)\cap B_{1})\geq K_{*}-o(1)
. Assume there were some
x\in\{|\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|\leq 0.9\}\cap B_{1/2}\setminus B_{r}(L)
for contradiction; then

K_{*}\geq\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}},B_{1})\geq\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}},B_{r/2}(L)\cap B_{1})+\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}},B_{r/2}(x)\cap B_{1})\geq K_{*}-o(1)+\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}},B_{r/2}(x)\cap B_{1})\,,

which (by lower density estimates around
x
, see Step 1 in the proof of
Lemma

4.5
) is a contradiction for
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}>0
small enough.

Case
d_{*}<n-1
.
By
Theorem

3.4
we have the sheeting assumptions away from
L
(as the bad set is contained in a
o(1)
tube around
L
). In particular, the sets
\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=0\}
enjoy
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates. By Arzelà–Ascoli—plus standard covering and diagonal arguments—we obtain some subsequence (not relabeled) converging (in
C^{2,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(B_{1}\setminus L)
) to a smooth minimal surface
\Sigma\subset B_{1}\setminus L
(with
\partial\Sigma\subset L\cup\partial B_{1}
). By the argument at the beginning of the section, the
C^{2,\alpha}_{\rm loc}
convergence implies moreover that
\Sigma
is stable away from
L
.

The following is immediate from (
4.10
):

Lemma B.2
.

There is
R_{0}
such that the following holds: Let
\delta>0
. Let
s,t
be such that
|s|,|t|\leq 0.9
. Then, given
x\in\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=s\}\cap B_{1}\setminus B_{\delta}(L)
, we have—for
l
large enough—that
\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}\cap B_{R_{0}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}(x)\neq\emptyset
as well.

In particular (by considering
s=0
), we deduce that
\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}
also converges both in the
C^{2,\alpha}
and Hausdorff senses to
\Sigma
in
B_{1}\setminus B_{\delta}(L)
(with a rate independent of
t
).

In particular,
\Sigma
must be nonempty: Otherwise, cover
B_{1}\cap B_{r}(L)
with
\sim r^{-d_{*}}
balls of radius
r>0
small; the contributions of these balls (using that
{\bf M}
is bounded) to the total energy in
B_{1}
amount to at most
Cr
. Moreover, by
Lemma

B.2
,
|u|\leq 0.9
is contained in this cover (for
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}
small enough), thus by
Lemma

4.3
the energy of
\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}
decays exponentially away from these balls. Making
r
small, the lower bound for the energy density in
B_{1}
(in fact, it is almost
K_{*}
) yields a contradiction.

Now, passing (
140
) to the limit,
\Sigma
is conical and invariant in any direction
e\in L
. Thus, in an appropriate frame
\Sigma=C\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{*}}
, where
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-d_{*}}
is a minimal cone which is smooth and stable outside the origin. We have then two subcases:

Subcase 1:
If
d_{*}\leq n-3
(thus
3\leq n-d_{*}\leq 7
),
C
(and thus
\Sigma
) is a hyperplane by
[
Sim68
]
.

Subcase 2:
If
d_{*}=n-2
,
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a union of half-lines intersecting at the origin. We argue as follows:

Lemma B.3
.

There exists
U_{i_{l}}\subset B_{1}^{n-2}
, with
{\rm Vol}_{n-2}(U_{i_{l}})\geq c_{0}>0
, such that for every
x_{0}\in U_{i_{l}}
and
t
with
|t|\leq 0.9
we have: Let
S_{y_{0},t}:=\{x:(x,y_{0})\in\{u=t\}\}\cap B_{1}^{2}
. Then
S_{y_{0},t}\neq\emptyset
, and if
x\in S_{y_{0},t}
then
\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}
is a smooth hypersurface around
(x,y_{0})
. Moreover,
|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|\geq\frac{c}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}
.

Proof.

The fact that
S_{y_{0},t}\neq\emptyset
is immediate by applying
Lemma

B.2
around any point in
\Sigma\cap[B_{1}\setminus B_{\frac{1}{10}}(L)]\cap\{y=y_{0}\}\neq\emptyset
. Now, let
\Pi_{n-2}
denote projection onto
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
, and define
U_{i_{l}}=B_{1}^{n-2}\setminus\Pi_{n-2}[B_{R_{0}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}(\widetilde{Z}_{i}\cap B_{4})]
,
R_{0}
universal to be chosen. By stability and Vitali,
B_{R_{0}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}(\widetilde{Z}_{i}\cap B_{4})
can be covered with at most
C\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}^{-(n-3)}
balls of radius
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}
. Projecting these balls,
{\rm Vol}_{n-2}(\Pi_{n-2}[B_{R_{0}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}(\widetilde{Z}_{i}\cap B_{4})])\leq C\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}^{-(n-3)}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}^{n-2}=C\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}
, which can be made arbitrarily small as
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\to 0
.

Fixing
R_{0}
(universal) large enough, for any
y_{0}\in U_{i_{l}}
we can apply
Theorem

3.4
(appropriately rescaled) in
B_{R_{0}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}(0,y_{0})
, thus (
14
) holds in
B_{\frac{R_{0}}{2}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}(0,y_{0})
. This gives
|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|\geq\frac{c}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}}
as desired. In particular, if
|t|\leq 0.9
, then
\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}
is a smooth hypersurface around
S_{y_{0},t}
.
∎

The rest is essentially the classical argument in
[
SS81
, pp. 785-787]
; we give a sketch of the proof, divided into two main lemmas. We have:

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\sigma>0
. Then, for all
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}>0
small enough, we have

\inf_{t\in[-0.9,0.9],\,y_{0}\in U_{i_{l}}}\int_{S_{y_{0},t}\cap B_{\sigma}^{2}}|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}|\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}\leq\sigma^{1/2}.

Proof.

By Holder’s inequality,
Proposition

2.4
and the boundedness of
{\bf M}
(on balls of radius
\sigma
), we easily see that

\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{\sigma}^{2}\times B_{1}^{n-2}}\mathcal{A}|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|^{2}\leq\left(\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{1}}\mathcal{A}^{2}|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{\sigma}^{2}\times B_{1}^{n-2}}|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq C\sigma^{1/2}.

On the other hand, by
Lemma

B.3
and the coarea formula, we can estimate

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}\int_{B_{\sigma}^{2}\times B_{1}^{n-2}}\mathcal{A}|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\{|\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|\leq 0.9\}\cap(B_{\sigma}^{2}\times U_{i_{l}})}\mathcal{A}|\nabla\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}|=\int_{-0.9}^{0.9}dt\int_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}\cap(U_{i_{l}}\times B_{\sigma}^{2})}\mathcal{A}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{-0.9}^{0.9}dt\int_{U_{i_{l}}}dy_{0}\int_{S_{y_{0},t}\cap B_{\sigma}^{2}}|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}|\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}\geq\inf_{t\in[-0.9,0.9],\,y_{0}\in U_{i_{l}}}\int_{S_{y_{0},t}\cap B_{\sigma}^{2}}|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}|\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}\,.

∎

On the other hand, we have:

Lemma B.5
.

Assume that
\Sigma
were not a hyperplane. Then,
\int_{S_{y_{0},t}\cap B_{\sigma}^{2}}|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}|\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}\geq c>0
for every
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}>0
small enough and
t\in[-0.9,0.9],\,y_{0}\in U_{i_{l}}
.

Proof.

By assumption,
\Sigma
is a union of half-planes intersecting at
\{0\}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
and which form some angle. Moreover,
\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}
is smoothly embedded and it converges in
C^{1}_{loc}
to
\Sigma
away from
\{0\}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
—thus, in particular, in
(B_{\sigma}^{2}\setminus B_{\sigma/2}^{2})\times B_{1}^{n-2}
.

By elementary considerations, for
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}>0
small enough there are then
x_{1},x_{2}\in B_{\sigma}^{2}\setminus B_{\sigma/2}^{2}
, both belonging to the same connected component of
S_{y_{0},t}\cap B_{\sigma}^{2}
, and such that
|\nu_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}(x_{1},y_{0})-\nu_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}(x_{2},y_{0})|\geq c
; in other words, the normal vector to
\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}
needs to rotate by a definite positive angle along some connected component of
S_{y_{0},t}
.

Let
\gamma\subset S_{y_{0},t}\cap B_{\sigma}^{2}
be a curve segment connecting
(x_{1},y_{0})
and
(x_{2},y_{0})
. Since
|\mathcal{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\{u=t\}}|
bounds any tangential derivative of the normal vector
\nu_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}
, the fundamental theorem of calculus then gives

|\nu_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}(x_{1},y_{0})-\nu_{\{\widetilde{u}_{i_{l}}=t\}}(x_{2},y_{0})|=\int_{\gamma}\frac{d}{d\gamma}\nu_{\{u=t\}}\leq\int_{S_{y_{0},t}\cap B_{\sigma}^{2}}|\mathcal{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\{u=t\}}|\,.

∎

Combining the two preceding lemmas we deduce that
\Sigma
must be a hyperplane, as otherwise fixing
\sigma>0
small enough and then taking
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{i_{l}}>0
small enough we get a contradiction.

Conclusion.
In either of the cases
d_{*}=n-1
and
d_{*}<n-1
, we found that
\Sigma
is a hyperplane. The first property in (
141
) immediately follows, by
Lemma

B.2
. To see the second one, let
r>0
. Since
\Sigma\setminus B_{2r}(L)
is connected, by
Lemma

4.9
we find some
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that
{\bf M}_{r}(x)=K+o(1)
for any
x\in\Sigma\setminus B_{2r}(L)
. But then necessarily
K=K_{*}
: This follows easily since
{\bf M}_{1}(0)=K_{*}+o(1)
, and (as we already saw)
B_{s}(L)
contributes at most
Cs
to the total energy, which can be made arbitrarily small.

Given that also
{\bf M}_{r}(y)=K_{*}+o(1)
for any
y\in L
, we easily conclude (by combining different values of
r
) that
{\bf M}_{r}(x)=K_{*}+o(1)
for
any

x\in\Sigma
, which is precisely the second property in (
141
).
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of some known or standard results

Lemmas
4.2
and
4.3
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

This can be proved as in
[
Wan17
, Remark 4.7]
. Given a vector field
X\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, multiplying (
5
) by
X\cdot\nabla u
and integrating by parts we get

\displaystyle\int\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}+W(u)\right]{\rm div}X-(\nabla u)^{T}\cdot DX\cdot\nabla u=0\,,

or (adding
\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}
on both sides):

\displaystyle\int\Big[{\rm div}X-\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\frac{u_{i}}{|\nabla u|}\frac{u_{j}}{|\nabla u|}\partial_{i}X^{j}\Big]|\nabla u|^{2}=\int\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}-W(u)\right]{\rm div}X\,.

We choose
X=x_{n}\eta^{2}e_{n}
, getting

\displaystyle\int\Big[\eta^{2}+2x_{n}\eta\eta_{n}-\frac{u_{n}}{|\nabla u|}\frac{u_{n}}{|\nabla u|}\eta^{2}-2\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\frac{u_{i}}{|\nabla u|}\frac{u_{n}}{|\nabla u|}x_{n}\eta\eta_{i}\Big]|\nabla u|^{2}=\int\left[\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}-W(u)\right](\eta^{2}+2x_{n}\eta\eta_{n})\,.

The first and third terms add up to
|\nabla^{e_{n}^{\prime}}u|^{2}\eta^{2}
. Moreover, the terms with
i<n
in the sum can be estimated by

2\sum_{i<n}u_{i}u_{n}x_{n}\eta\eta_{i}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i<n}[u_{i}^{2}\eta^{2}+4u_{n}^{2}x_{n}^{2}\eta_{i}^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{2}|\nabla^{e_{n}^{\prime}}u|^{2}\eta^{2}+2x_{n}^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\,.

Bringing all remaining terms to the right hand side and using
\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{2}\leq W(u)
by
Lemma

2.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int|\nabla^{e_{n}^{\prime}}u|^{2}\eta^{2}\leq 4\int x_{n}^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}|\nabla\eta|^{2}+C\int W(u)|x_{n}\eta\eta_{n}|

as desired.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.3
.

Using
Lemma

2.1
, we can compute

\displaystyle\Delta(1-u^{2})=-2[|\nabla u|^{2}+uW^{\prime}(u)]\geq-2[2W(u)+uW^{\prime}(u)]=-2(1-u^{2})[\frac{1}{4}(1-u^{2})-u^{2}].

For
|u|\geq 0.85
we find that
(1-u^{2})
is a strong subsolution, i.e.

\displaystyle\Delta(1-u^{2})\geq c(1-u^{2})

(we can actually put
c=1
). By the maximum principle (using an exponential for comparison), this shows that

\displaystyle\sup_{B_{r}(x)}1-u^{2}\leq e^{-c_{0}r}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{in any}\hskip 10.00002ptB_{2r}(x)\subset\{|u|\geq 0.85\}\,,

which immediately gives the bound for
W(u)
;
Lemma

2.1
then bounds the gradient term as well.
∎

Lemma
7.9
.

We first need:

Lemma C.1

(
W^{1,1}
estimate
)
.

Assume that
v\in C^{2}(B_{2R}^{\prime})
,
B_{2R}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. Set
f:={\rm div}(A\nabla v)
, where
A\in C^{1}(B_{2R}^{\prime})
and
|A(x^{\prime})-1|\leq 1/2
. Then, there exists
C
depending only on
n
such that

R\fint_{B_{R}^{\prime}}|\nabla v|\leq C\left(R^{2}\fint_{B_{2R}^{\prime}}|f|+\fint_{B_{2R}^{\prime}}|v|\right).

Proof of
Lemma

C.1
.

Since the estimate is scaling invariant we can (and do) assume that
R=1
. Consider
w
which solves

\begin{cases}{\rm div}(Aw)&=f\quad\mbox{in}\ B_{2}^{\prime}\\
w&=0\quad\mbox{on}\ \partial B_{2}^{\prime}\,.\end{cases}

Since
1/2\leq A(x^{\prime})\leq 3/2
is bounded and uniformly elliptic,
[
LSW63
, Theorem 5.1]
shows that

\|w\|_{L^{1}(B_{2})}+\|\nabla w\|_{L^{1}(B_{2})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{2})}\,.

Moreover,
v-w
satisfies
{\rm div}(A\nabla(v-w))=0
, therefore (applying Cauchy–Schwarz, the Caccioppoli inequality, and De Giorgi–Nash–Moser estimates)

\|\nabla(v-w)\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}^{\prime})}\leq C\|\nabla(v-w)\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{\prime})}\leq C\|v-w\|_{L^{2}(B_{3/2}^{\prime})}\leq C\|v-w\|_{L^{1}(B_{2}^{\prime})}\,.

Bounding
\|v-w\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}^{\prime})}\leq\|v\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}^{\prime})}+\|w\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}^{\prime})}\leq\|v\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}^{\prime})}+\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{2}^{\prime})}
, and adding up the gradient estimates for
w
and
v-w
above, we conclude the result.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

7.9
.

We argue by compactness/contradiction. Suppose that the statement is not true; then, there are sequences
v_{k},A_{k}
satisfying the previous hypotheses for
\delta_{k}\downarrow 0
but such that the conclusion fails for a certain
\lambda>0
.

Applying
Lemma

C.1
rescaled to the ball of radius
\rho
we see that

\rho\fint_{B_{\rho}^{\prime}}|\nabla v_{k}|\leq\rho^{d+1/2}
, for
1\leq\rho\leq\tfrac{1}{2\delta_{k}}
. We can then apply Rellich–Kondrachov, deducing then that a subsequence (not relabeled) of the
v_{k}
converges strongly in
L_{loc}^{1}
, and weakly in
W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}
, to some
v_{\infty}
, and with growth bound
\fint_{B_{\rho}}|v_{\infty}|\,dx\leq\rho^{d+1/2}
.

Now, for any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
, the previous together with
|A_{k}(x^{\prime})-1|\leq\delta_{k}\to 0
give that

\int v_{\infty}\Delta\varphi=\lim_{k}\int v_{k}\Delta\varphi=\lim_{k}\int\nabla v_{k}\nabla\varphi=\lim_{k}\int A_{k}\nabla v_{k}\nabla\varphi=\lim_{k}\int{\rm div}(A_{k}\nabla v_{k})\varphi=0\,,

thus
v_{\infty}
is smooth and harmonic by the Weyl Lemma.

By the standard Liouville-type theorem for harmonic functions in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
with polynomial growth,
v_{\infty}
must be a harmonic polynomial
p_{d}
of degree
\leq d
. Moreover, from the growth bound with
\rho=1
we see that
\|p_{d}\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}^{\prime})}\leq|B_{1}^{\prime}|
. Since
v_{k}\to v_{\infty}=p_{d}
in
L^{1}(B_{1}^{\prime})
, this gives a contradiction for
k
large enough.
∎

Appendix D
Asymptotic behaviour of 1D periodic Allen–Cahn solutions

Let
\alpha\in[0,1]
and

P(\alpha,R):=\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}\left(v,[0,\frac{1+\alpha}{4}\sqrt{2}\log{R}]\right):v\in C_{c}^{1}([0,\frac{1+\alpha}{4}\sqrt{2}\log{R}])\text{ and }v(0)=0\right\}.

Proposition D.1
.

There are universal constants
C>0
and
R_{0}>1
such that
P(\alpha,R)\geq\frac{1}{2}-CR^{-(1+\alpha)}
for every
R\geq R_{0}
.

Considering

P(R):=\inf\left\{\mathcal{E}\left(v,[0,\log{R}]\right):v\in C_{c}^{1}([0,\log{R}])\text{ and }v(0)=0\right\},

(142)

so that
P(\alpha,R)=P(R^{\frac{1+\alpha}{4}\sqrt{2}})
,
it suffices to show:

Proposition D.2
.

There are universal constants
C>0
and
R_{0}>1
such that
P(R)\geq\frac{1}{2}-CR^{-2\sqrt{2}}
for every
R\geq R_{0}
.

This should be read as: Any function with
v(0)=0
needs to accumulate almost as much A–C energy as the 1D solution
\phi
in long intervals. Indeed, we have:

Lemma D.3
.

P(R)\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

The monotone 1D solution
\phi
is a competitor in (
142
), and it has
\mathcal{E}(\phi,[0,\infty))=\frac{1}{2}
, since
\mathcal{E}(\phi,(-\infty,\infty))=1
and
\phi
is antisymmetric.
∎

We argue in several steps.

Lemma D.4
.

There is
R_{0}>1
such that, if
R>R_{0}
, the following hold:

The infimum in (
142
) is attained by an A–C solution
v\in C_{c}^{1}([0,\log{R}])
, satisfying
v(0)=0
,
v^{\prime}(0)>0
, and
v^{\prime}(\log{R})=0
. Moreover,
v
is the restriction of a (not renamed) global, periodic A–C solution
v:\mathbb{R}\to(-1,1)
with quarter-period
\frac{T}{4}=\log{R}
. Furthermore,
v
is nondecreasing in
[0,\frac{T}{4}]
.

Proof.

It is elementary to see that there is an
v
which attains the infimum. Since
v
solves a minimisation problem, with zero Dirichlet condition at
0
and no constraint at
\log{R}
, we get that
v(0)=0
and
v^{\prime}(\log{R})=0
. Moreover,
v^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
(as otherwise
v\equiv 0
, but then
P(R)=\int_{0}^{\log R}W(0)=\frac{1}{4}\log R
, which is a contradiction for
R
large enough with
Lemma

D.3
), so that
v^{\prime}(0)>0
up to perhaps considering
-v
instead.

Since
v
is a minimizer of the A–C energy in
[0,\log R]
among all functions with
v(0)=0
and
v^{\prime}(0)>0
, and since the potential
W
is decreasing in
[0,1]
and
W(1)=0
, we obtain that:

•

v
is nondecreasing in
[0,\log R]
(as otherwise the smallest nondecreasing function above
v
would have less energy).

•

v\leq 1
(as otherwise the function
\min(v,1)
would have less energy).

Moreover, a symmetrisation by hand alternating even and odd reflections (using that
v^{\prime}(\log R)=0
) immediately shows that
v
can be extended periodically, with quarter period
\frac{T}{4}=\log{R}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Lemma D.5
.

In
[0,\frac{T}{4}]
,
v^{\prime}=\sqrt{2W(v)-2\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, so that
v
coincides with the solution to

\begin{cases}u_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}=W^{\prime}(u_{\lambda})\\
u_{\lambda}(0)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad u_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}-2\lambda}.\\
\end{cases}

(143)

Moreover,
v
has amplitude
C_{\lambda}:=\sqrt{1-2\sqrt{\lambda}}
, attained at
\frac{T}{4}
.

Proof.

Since

\frac{d}{dt}\left(v^{\prime 2}-2W(v)\right)=2v^{\prime}v^{\prime\prime}-2v^{\prime}W^{\prime}(v)=0

and
v^{\prime}>0
in
[0,\frac{T}{4})
,

v^{\prime}=\sqrt{2W(u)-2\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
in this interval. Since
|v^{\prime}|\leq\sqrt{2W(v)}
by
Lemma

2.1
,
\lambda>0
. If
\lambda=0
we would get
v=\phi
, but
\phi^{\prime}
never vanishes; since
|W|\leq\frac{1}{4}
we deduce that
\lambda\in(0,1/4)
. Moreover, since
v
attains its maximum
\max v
at
\frac{T}{4}
and
v^{\prime}(\frac{T}{4})=0
, we see that
0=\sqrt{2W(\max v)-2\lambda}
, which since
W(v)=\frac{1}{4}(1-v^{2})^{2}
shows that
\max v=v(T/4)=\sqrt{1-\sqrt{4\lambda}}
.
∎

We will also need the following

Lemma D.6
.

We have

\displaystyle P(R)\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}\int_{C_{\lambda}}^{1}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds.

Proof.

Recall that
\frac{T_{\lambda}}{4}=\log R
. By
a^{2}+b^{2}\geq 2ab
and coarea, we can bound

\displaystyle P(R)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}\int_{0}^{\frac{T_{\lambda}}{4}}\left[\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u_{\lambda}|^{2}+W(u_{\lambda})\right]\geq\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}\int_{0}^{\frac{T_{\lambda}}{4}}|\nabla u_{\lambda}|\sqrt{2W(u_{\lambda})}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}\int_{0}^{C_{\lambda}}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds\,,

which since
\sigma_{0}=\int_{-1}^{1}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds=2\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds
gives

\displaystyle P(R)\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}\int_{C_{\lambda}}^{1}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds\,.

∎

We can now prove
Proposition

D.2
.

Proof of
Proposition

D.2
.

Let
\delta=\delta_{\lambda}
be such that
C_{\lambda}=u_{\lambda}(T_{\lambda}/4)=1-\delta
. We want to see that
\delta
is small.

Recall that
u_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\sqrt{2W(u_{\lambda})-\lambda}
.
Dividing by
\sqrt{2W(u_{\lambda})-\lambda}
, integrating from
0
to
\frac{T_{\lambda}}{4}
, and setting
s=u(r)
gives

\frac{T_{\lambda}}{4}=\int_{0}^{\frac{T_{\lambda}}{4}}\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{\sqrt{2W(u(r))-2\lambda}}\,dr=\int_{0}^{1-\delta}\frac{ds}{\sqrt{2W(s)-2W(1-\delta)}}\,,

(144)

where we used that (since
u_{\lambda}^{\prime}(T_{\lambda}/4)=0
) we have
2\lambda=2W(u_{\lambda}(T_{\lambda}/4))=2W(1-\delta)
.

Changing variables
s\xrightarrow{}1-s
, bounding
2-s\geq 2-\delta
, and changing variables
s\xrightarrow{}\delta s
, we can estimate

\begin{split}\frac{T}{4}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{0}^{1-\delta}\frac{ds}{\sqrt{W(s)-W(1-\delta)}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\delta}^{1}\frac{ds}{\sqrt{(2-s)^{2}s^{2}-(2-\delta)^{2}\delta^{2}}}\\
&\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2-\delta}\int_{\delta}^{1}\frac{ds}{\sqrt{s^{2}-\delta^{2}}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2-\delta}\int_{1}^{1/\delta}\frac{ds}{\sqrt{s^{2}-1}}\,.\end{split}

Therefore, since
\frac{1}{\sqrt{s^{2}-1}}=\frac{1}{s}+O(\frac{1}{s^{2}})
for
s\geq 2
, we conclude that

\frac{T}{4}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2-\delta}\int_{2}^{1/\delta}\frac{ds}{s}+O(1)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}+O(1)\,.

Since
T/4=\log R
, exponentiating on both sides we deduce that
\delta\leq CR^{-\sqrt{2}}
.

Now that we have bounded
\delta
, since

\displaystyle P(R)\geq\frac{1}{2}-\int_{C_{\lambda}}^{1}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds,

it remains to estimate
\int_{C_{\lambda}}^{1}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds
. From
\sqrt{2W(s)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1-s^{2})\leq C(1-s)
for
s\in[0,1]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{C_{\lambda}}^{1}\sqrt{2W(s)}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{C_{\lambda}}^{1}(1-s)\,ds=\frac{C}{2}(1-C_{\lambda})^{2}=\frac{C}{2}\delta^{2}\leq CR^{-2\sqrt{2}},

and the proposition follows.
∎
